analysis of an intermittent gate dielectric leakage observed in the SRAM array of a FinFETbased technology. The study combines conventional electrical nanoprobing methods that include a novel data interpretation technique referred to as Null-Current Voltage Determination. In contrast to other papers that rely on artificially induced gate leakage failures for retrospective analysis, this work captures real-time evidence of genuine intermittent gate leakage originating from the metal-gate dielectric. Initial electrical failure analysis (EFA) using atomic force probing (AFP) did not reveal any physical defects, resulting in a No Defect Found (NDF) classification. In response, an alternative strategy was employed involving pico-current scanning and voltage ramping beyond nominal operating levels. This methodology successfully revealed an intermittent leakage event at the VDD (source) terminal of the pull-up (PU) transistor within the defective SRAM bit cell, suggesting a gate reliability issue. In addition, the application of the Null-Current Voltage Determination technique on devices exhibiting elevated gate leakage enabled differentiation of the leakage path, whether it was directed toward the source\/drain epi region or into the channel silicon. These insights enabled meaningful corrective measures, ultimately improving product quality.<\/jats:p>","DOI":"10.31399\/asm.cp.istfa2025p0375","type":"proceedings-article","page":"375-380","title":["Analysis and Characterization of Intermittent Gate Leakage in a FinFET increases, the importance of failure analysis continues to grow for improving product yield. In addition, various new types of defects are being identified as transistor devices evolve from planar to three-dimensional structures such as FINFETs or GAAFETs. Accordingly, it is important to quickly resolve known defects and to identify new defects, as well as to accurately pinpoint defect locations to reduce the time required for physical failure analysis (PFA). In this paper, a closed-loop failure analysis framework integrating scan diagnosis, inline defect inspection data, and design hotspots for rapid and effective yield improvement is proposed and validated. Through correlation with supporting defect data, the proposed method enables the pre-identification of defect types and their characteristics within the product and accurately pinpoints defect locations. The proposed method further enables reuse of PFA-validated weak points through improving rule database (RDB) and supports systematic defect monitoring via FMA-driven trending. Validation on practical production data demonstrated substantial improvement in FA efficiency, including reduction in PFA turnaround time over 80%, from approximately 4 hours to 30 minutes per The two workflows are evaluated based on TEM sample quality, site-specific targeting ability, and other operational aspects important for their application within the semiconductor industry. For most devices, both workflows produce excellent TEM samples that are comparable with respect to TEM image quality and feature resolution. For certain devices, however, one workflow can be superior to the other. For example, the FIB workflow is preferred for devices sensitive to mechanical damage. The superior targeting ability achievable in the dual-beam instrument enables the production of site-specific samples with single-transistor precision, which is not possible with the manual process. Alternatively, manual samples are advantageous if a large field of view or multiple features within a singular cross-section plane with varying degrees of sample thickness are required. In terms of operational aspects, the FIB-based workflow is faster overall, allows for better site-specific targeting, requires fewer dies, reduces the amount of instrument operator time needed, and requires less training time compared to the manual process.<\/jats:p>","DOI":"10.31399\/asm.cp.istfa2025p0275","type":"proceedings-article","page":"275-283","title":["Comparison of Manual and        <jats:p>Anomaly detection has been increasingly recognized as a crucial aspect in the semiconductor manufacturing industry, significantly contributing to the improvement of yield management and crack origin remains a significant challenge in semiconductor failure analysis due to the complex fracture behavior within multilayer package structures. Conventional Scanning Acoustic Microscopy (SAM) C-scan inspection is effective for crack detection; however, it provides limited information regarding crack initiation location, propagation depth, and internal fracture behavior in complex package structures. In this study, multi-mode SAM analysis combining C-scan, B-scan, and TAMI (Tomographic Acoustic Micro-Imaging) was used to characterize die crack propagation behavior and identify crack origins in TSOP, BGA, and WLCSP packages. Simulated mechanical denting conditions were applied to both top-side and bottom-side package surfaces to generate controlled crack propagation under bending stress conditions. A Time-of-Flight (TOF)-based depth correlation methodology was developed using calibrated material acoustic velocity values to estimate crack propagation depth across different material interfaces. The results demonstrated that different crack origins produce distinctive acoustic signatures and fracture propagation behaviors. Top-side denting generated localized and asymmetric crack morphology, while bottom-side denting produced broader vertical fracture propagation with severe internal silicon damage extending toward the die backside.      <jats:p>Scanning acoustic microscopy (SAM) is a standard technique for the non-destructive inspection of semiconductor packages. While highly effective, the axial resolution of SAM images is naturally influenced by the transducer's impulse response; device-specific properties such as aperture and focal length broaden the acoustic pulse, causing echoes from closely spaced layers to merge into a single image. This signal overlap makes it difficult to cleanly isolate specific depths within calculation gates. Furthermore, because these pulse-broadening effects vary across different hardware setups, the resulting data lacks the consistency required to reliably transfer machine learning models between systems. We present a deconvolution framework that enhances signal clarity by recovering the underlying acoustic reflectivity sequence and suppressing these transducer-dependent characteristics. The model is trained on semi-synthetic data derived from recorded point-spread functions (PSFs), mapping measured signals to a more impulsive representation. This work presents an integrated sMIM platform that addresses these challenges through three key innovations. First, a novel RF detection architecture utilizing a high dynamic range RF baseband eliminates the need for hardware cancellation circuitry, enabling high-sensitivity operation using standard sharp conductive cantilevers. Second, real-time background suppression is achieved via distance-synchronized differential sMIM measurements, removing stray capacitance and drift during raster scanning without requiring post-processing. Third, a simplified phase calibration method directly maps in-phase and quadrature signals into capacitive and resistive microwave responses, enabling instantaneous, single-point digital calibration.           <jats:p>Advanced semiconductor packaging technologies such as heterogeneous integration, chiplets, high-bandwidth memory, and three-dimensional integrated circuits (3D ICs) require non-destructive imaging techniques capable of resolving dense buried interconnect structures. This work investigates few-shot segmentation of semiconductor X-ray cross-sectional imagery acquired using computed tomography (CT) and precision angle laminography (PAL) under varying acquisition conditions. A prompt-based few-shot segmentation framework, referred to as FSS-SAM3, was implemented using the Segment Anything Model 3 operating entirely at inference time without gradient-based fine-tuning. Experiments were conducted on a dataset collected from NVIDIA H100 advanced packaging chiplets containing annotated C4 and microbump structures acquired under multiple laminographic slant-angle configurations. Quantitative evaluation under 1-shot and 5-shot protocols demonstrated substantial segmentation improvement for sparse interconnect structures, with C4 segmentation increasing from 29.19% to 65.03% mIoU when additional support examples were introduced. Comparative analysis between CT and PAL additionally demonstrated differences in reconstruction fidelity, artifact behavior, and interconnect visibility across varying acquisition geometries and slant angles. While the proposed framework showed strong correspondence-learning capability for sparse semiconductor structures, dense repetitive microbump arrays remained challenging due to extreme instance density and limited prompt propagation across the query image. The results demonstrate the potential of foundation-model-based few-shot segmentation for semiconductor failure analysis while highlighting key challenges associated with dense-instance segmentation        <jats:p>Photon Emission Microscopy (PEM) is a popular failure analysis technique capable of detecting photons emitted from defective circuitry and helps pinpoint the failure location. This technique has helped identify open defects, polysilicon gate defects, gate oxide-related failures, electrostatic discharge failures, metallization failures, logic state detection, memory bit failures, and leakage failures. In this paper, we discuss how photon emission microscopy can be a valuable tool in identifying and understanding intrinsic device reliability issues. We examine how emission intensity, measured by processing photon emission images using a Python script and an open-source computer vision tool, can be used to estimate the relative number of trapped charges compared to leakage over time under applied stress. Through case studies, we show how photon emission microscopy (PEM) can reveal interactions between design processes and different IP blocks within a microelectronic chip. <jats:p>Accurate transmission electron microscopy (TEM) characterization of photoresist (PR) is limited by preparation-induced artifacts that distort native morphology and obscure failure mechanisms, leading to unreliable root-cause identification in advanced-node failure analysis. We introduce an approach that decouples PR characterization from these artifacts by applying an ultra-thin (~10\u201320 \u00c5) conformal CVD oxide deposited near room temperature (~30 \u00b0C). The oxide stabilizes the PR throughout FIB lamella preparation and low-dose TEM imaging, suppressing deformation, charging, and shrinkage while preserving intrinsic morphology. We benchmarked this method against common protection strategies, including M-Bond, standard Pt and Ir coatings, and e-beam-assisted deposition. All produced pronounced PR deformation and profile distortion from preparation and beam interaction rather than the native structure. In contrast, the low-temperature oxide improved profile measurement accuracy by ~7% and achieved a TEM imaging success rate near 99%. By removing artifact-driven ambiguity, the method reveals intrinsic process-induced PR damage that conventional workflows can mask or misinterpret. In a production case with a 12.5% yield loss, the distortion-free evidence enabled accurate root cause identification, redirected process development, and reduced yield loss to 0.3%. SSN architecture poses major challenges for Laser Voltage Probing (LVP) and Soft Defect Localization (SDL) for at-speed and stuck-at tests. The loop setup strategies that work for older DFT architectures are no longer applicable for SSN based products. Traditional detection and prevention methods like testing and side-channel analysis have limitations in reliability and scalability. Automated reverse engineering by deep learning (DL) models is a foolproof approach to hardware assurance, but faces challenges due to limited data. By pooling data from different stakeholders (competitors in industry, governments, etc.), DL models can be more effectively trained but privacy of intellectual property (IP) is a significant concern. Federated Learning (FL) has been proposed as a potential alternative allowing for the collaborative training of a DL model without sharing raw data. While FL has been widely used in healthcare, IoT, and finance, its application in hardware assurance remains underexplored. This study investigates, for the first time, FL-based DL for hardware assurance, demonstrating that FL outperforms single-client centralized learning in segmentation tasks for reverse engineering. Our results show that increasing the number of clients improves FL performance by collaboratively training the model with more data.        <jats:p>This paper investigates the application of Electron Energy-Loss Near-Edge Structure (ELNES) analysis as a powerful tool for identifying defects in advanced semiconductor devices. Analysis of the ELNES region of an Electron Energy-Loss Spectroscopy (EELS) spectrum reveals chemical bonding characteristics and oxidation states, providing information beyond basic elemental identification. This methodology includes high-resolution ELNES data acquisition using a 200kV probe-corrected, cold-FEG TEM equipped with a post-column EELS spectrometer. With the use of advanced instrumentation - such as aberration correctors, monochromators, and more recently direct detection cameras - EELS can now be performed with superior energy resolution and sub-nanometer spatial resolution. The technique is demonstrated using the Si L2,3-edge in a FinFET technology. Results demonstrate the effectiveness of ELNES analysis in distinguishing different oxides via a fingerprinting technique, highlighting its ability to identify potential barrier layer anomalies. ELNES analysis can also be applied to investigations of oxidation-related anomalies in gate stacks, contamination or oxidation during manufacturing processes, interfacial degradation in multilayer structures, among other failure mechanisms. As semiconductor devices continue to increase in complexity, ELNES provides not just complementary insight, but in some cases, the only viable method for identifying bonding-related anomalies at the atomic scale. However, Electrical Fault Isolation (EFI) techniques face serious challenges, not only with Critical Dimension issues but also with excessive power consumption. Photo Emission Microscopy, Soft Defect Localization [1], Electro-Optical Probing, also called Laser Voltage Imaging [2], and Thermal Frequency Imaging [3], are widely used. These major EFI methods are usually performed under the ATE-driven operating environment. Recent scan-designed LSIs consume a large amount of current during tests, which never occurs in their normal operation. This current can disrupt EFI operations. For example, it could cause switching delays due to internal IR drops, leading to unexpected malfunctions in logic operations. Moreover, it may damage the device. To mitigate this issue, minimizing the amount of switching activity during tests is a widely adopted measure to reduce power consumption. However, when the switching activity is reduced, the total test time becomes longer, and so does the looping period for EFI. This weakens every EFI detection signal, causing the 2D location image to be less clear. To address this problem, a PEM with halting at failing logic state was studied, which will not cause excessive switching current.        <jats:p>Advanced microelectronics failure analysis increasingly requires rapid access to buried structures in heterogeneous material stacks, with surface quality suitable for follow-up with optical, confocal, SEM, EDS, FIB, TEM, or X-ray microscopy. Conventional workflows trade off preparation speed, accessible area, and artifact control. FIB offers excellent site specificity and nanoscale finishing but is inefficient for bulk removal, millimeter-scale cross sections, and multi-site investigations. We present a laser-first workflow in which ultrashort-pulsed laser processing performs high-throughput bulk removal and FIB is reserved for localized polishing or final high-resolution preparation. Two process controls enable this: a removable laser protective layer (LPL) that suppresses peripheral beam-tail interactions and protects adjacent surfaces, and intelligent multigas processing, which controls gas delivery during and between scan steps to reduce redeposition and aid debris evacuation. Demonstrations include large-area cross-sectioning, controlled package opening and delayering, access-hole formation, and TEM lamella precursor fabrication. Combined LPL and gas-assisted processing produced SEM-inspectable package cross sections with clear layer visibility across mixed stacks. Using representative removal rates, laser bulk removal reduces large-volume preparation from impractical FIB-only timescales to minutes. Confocal Scanning Acoustic Microscopy (CSAM) is an effective technique for inspecting TIM coverage due to its high sensitivity to interfacial separations. <jats:p>This study, build on the proof-of-concept work presented in ISTFA 2023 [1], demonstrates the implementation of e-beam probing on the latest TSMC A16 Back-Side Power Delivery Network (BSPDN) devices. In the new BSPDN design, routing congestion is reduced, allowing higher logic density with cheaper metallization lithography and power delivery is enhanced with improved performance per Watt. Structure wise, there are more than 15 metal layers above the device layer and power distribution layers below the silicon wafer. It is impossible for optical light to pass through and stimulate the defect as effective as the conventional optical fault isolation techniques (Figure 1). E-beam probing is not new. Back in 1990s, technology node was in micron scale and metal layer was few, e-beam probing at the frontside \u00b5m size metal traces with device stimulated by tester was the popular solution to functional failures [2]. The arrival of BSPDN architecture brings e-beam probing back to stage with the advantage of much better resolution than the optical probing techniques. With proper solutions in sample preparation that makes an area accessible for e-beam to travel, it is possible to perform e-beam probing through either the frontside or backside of the DUT (Figure 2). In the e-beam workflow development, several challenges were encountered. The complexity of metal signals led to issues such as crosstalk, charging, and contamination buildup due to prolonged e-beam exposure. The solution is to take advantage of e-beam pulsing function. Appropriate data collection methods were developed for Electron Voltage Imaging (EVI) and Electron Voltage Probing (EVP). For example, A continuous-wave (CW) e-beam scan was used for phase mapping (Phase EVI) to highlight the actuation circuit, while a pulsed e-beam was employed to measure the waveform (EVP) with reduced contamination or crosstalk. However, there is a significant disparity between the behavior predicted by the fault models used in diagnosis and the actual behavior observed in failing circuits. This undermines the performance of diagnosis methodologies that rely on conventional fault models to characterize defect behavior and deters downstream tasks such as physical failure analysis. Recently, a parameterizable fault model called PEPR has demonstrated the ability to bound defect behavior, even when it deviates from conventional fault model assumptions. Additionally, a novel diagnosis methodology called CHEF has demonstrated the application of PEPR to diagnosis to determine (i) the physical location of a defect and (ii) the precise changes to the logic functionality of the affected circuit in the form of a custom fault model. This paper presents an extension to CHEF (called SPICY CHEF) that better localizes and characterizes failure-causing defects using transistor-level SPICE simulation. Injected defects and their properties (location, impedance, etc.) that produce SPICE simulation responses that match the custom fault model generated by CHEF are reported as potential root causes of failure.      <jats:p>Inspection techniques of non-destructive character are highly relevant in a variety of fields in scientific and industrial applications. Conventional approaches such as chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) and plasma focused ion beam (FIB) milling often suffer from process-induced damage, surface contamination, and material-dependent milling variability, all of which can critically degrade the reliability of passive voltage contrast (PVC) observation. In this work, we present a newly developed air-free low-angle ion milling-scanning electron microscopy (LIM-SEM) platform, in which a LIM system is directly integrated into the sample exchange chamber of a high-resolution SEM, enabling uninterrupted delayering and observation without atmospheric exposure. Using this platform, direct delayering from the under-bump metallization (UBM) layer was successfully performed without any mechanical pre-treatment. The method provided uniform, wide-area, and damage-free material removal within practical processing times. The resulting surfaces remained free of contamination and mechanical artifacts, allowing stable SEM imaging from low magnifications up to beyond 100kx magnification. A direct comparison between air-exposed and air-free conditions clearly demonstrates that even short atmospheric exposure causes noticeable PVC degradation due to surface contamination, whereas the air-free workflow effectively preserves intrinsic voltage contrast. As a result, PVC visualization in advanced-node (7 nm) SRAM devices was substantially enhanced. These results demonstrate that the proposed LIM-SEM platform enables reliable visualization of intrinsic delayering behavior and voltage contrast in advanced semiconductor devices that are high-sensitive to oxidation and contamination. The air-free LIM-SEM platform provides a powerful and practical solution for next-generation failure analysis, establishing a highly reliable methodology for preserving surface integrity and enhancing        <jats:p>Transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and related characterization techniques are widely used to inspect semiconductor devices. The lack of direct optical access to the device's FET layer severely impacts techniques such as laser voltage probing and imaging (collectively, LVX), photon emission microscopy (PEM), and dynamic and static laser stimulation (DLS, SLS). Electron beam probing (EBP) is actively being developed to replace LVX for BPD ICs. It offers a small spot size, narrow probe geometry, long working distance, and, crucially, the ability to measure voltage signals from both nets and cells. EBP, however, requires direct line of sight access to any nets\/cells of interest, so its usefulness is heavily dependent on the ability to reach those nets\/cells using focused ion beam (FIB) created holes. Most high-performance compute ICs will have more than 10 frontside metal layers. With local routing confined to the lowermost levels, reaching most signals of interest will be difficult. As a result, EBP on BPD ICs will require careful planning and searching for the best locations to create FIB access holes. In this work, we present a novel methodology that leverages design for test (DFT) data and simulation to identify optimal frontside FIB access points. Locations with access unobstructed by signal nets are preferred, but rare, with &amp;lt;20% for the designs that were analyzed thus far. The main new development in this work is the capability of differentiating between relevant nets for the test in question vs. nets that can be safely removed while maintaining the ability to stimulate the faulty logic for EBP. This increases the chances for FIB access upto 45%.      <jats:p>For thin films with thickness of a few nanometers, information on thicknesses of the interfaces between adjacent layers is complimentary to the thickness of the layers themselves.      <jats:p>Passive voltage contrast (PVC) imaging using scanning electron microscopy (SEM) is a key technique for semiconductor failure analysis; however, continued device scaling to the nanometer regime has made it increasingly difficult to obtain high-resolution, high-contrast images under low accelerating voltage conditions. Conventional approaches often employ semi-in-lens configurations and\/or specimen biasing, but challenges remain in achieving stable and practical PVC imaging for advanced devices. In this study, we present a bias-free PVC observation method using a compound lens SEM that superposes magnetic and electrostatic fields, enabling stable imaging with reduced sensitivity to specimen topography. To further enhance performance at ultra-low accelerating voltages, the in-lens detector (ILD) was optimized in a JSM-IT810SHL system by improving the effective signal detection region for low-energy secondary electrons. Experiments were conducted at accelerating voltages down to 0.2 kV with a working distance of 1 mm and low probe current, targeting a 3 nm process SRAM device. The optimized ILD significantly improved detection efficiency for low-energy electrons in the 4-8 eV range to approximately 80%, resulting in clear PVC images without degradation even at ultra-low voltage. In addition, enhanced signal intensity improved live image visibility and enabled stable automatic imaging adjustments. The workflow combines X-ray computed tomography, AI-assisted correlative registration, femtosecond-laser sample preparation, previously demonstrated sensor-enabled endpointing concepts, Random-Forest-based recipe modeling, and automated image-analytics modules based on prior synthetic-data-enabled segmentation work. The paper is framed as a system-level vision supported by early demonstrations. An IC with BGA was imaged by X-ray CT, registered to the optical modality of the FemtoChisel laser system, and used to access a user-selected buried region with single-digit micrometer targeting accuracy. Systematic recipe optimization for laser processing is discussed as a structured-data pathway toward AI-assisted process selection, supported by demonstrations in glass\/display processing and prior random-forest-based process interpretation. Finally, previously demonstrated endpointing and synthetic-data-enabled image analytics are discussed as enabling modules for future closed-loop FA. Together, these demonstrations show how AI-assisted registration, laser access, process optimization, and image analytics can reduce manual iteration, preserve context across modalities, and This work presents the effects of varying electron beam energies on EBAC image quality and subsurface sensitivity. By acquiring EBAC images at multiple accelerating voltages, we explore how beam energy influences current collection efficiency, resolution, and the depth of electrical interaction within complex interconnect stacks. Lower beam energies emphasize surface-level features and near-surface opens, while higher energies enable visualization of deeper conductive layers and buried defects. A novel 3D reconstruction methodology is introduced, integrating multi-energy EBAC datasets to spatially resolve conductive pathways in three dimensions. This approach enhances defect localization accuracy in advanced nodes and supports root cause analysis for complex failure mechanisms such as via voids, leakage paths, and metal line breaks. However, curtain effects in the cross section and the formation of a surface damage layer remain major obstacles to accurate analysis. To improve milling performance, this study presents a needle-based shadow masking technique for PFIB milling to enhance milling performance during semiconductor sample preparation. By physically blocking the beam tail near the Region Of Interest (ROI), the proposed method minimizes surface damage and curtain effects without requiring protective layers. Cross-sectional milling experiments using the shadow masking technique significantly reduced the surface damage layer and curtain effects. Three key concerns from wafer fabrication, assembly, and failure analysis engineering teams are addressed: (A) whether galvanic corrosion is contamination-related; (B) whether galvanic corrosion originates in wafer fabrication or assembly processes; and (C) how defect characteristics differ between defects originating in wafer fabrication versus assembly.      <jats:p>Modern integrated circuits (ICs) increasingly incorporate advanced packaging materials and complex architectures that challenge conventional failure analysis (FA) techniques, particularly during decapsulation and sample preparation. This study addresses these challenges for a power switch device packaged with Sumitomo G712, a highly cross-linked, acid-resistant molding compound. Although G712 molding compound has been successfully decapsulated in previous work, the response observed in this device was significantly different. Under conventional mixed-acid decapsulation conditions, the molding compound exhibited increased resistance to etching, resulting in incomplete material removal. This indicates that the effective etching behavior is influenced not only by the material itself but also by package architecture and device-specific conditions, which can limit acid interaction and accessibility. A systematic evaluation of decapsulation recipes identified insufficient chemical contact time as the primary limitation of automated processes. An optimized extended-contact chemical approach was developed, enabling effective molding compound removal while maintaining wire integrity, as verified by wire pull testing (WPT). A separate controlled mechanical backside preparation method was also developed for the complex multi-material structure, including molding compound, copper heatsink, and ceramic layers. Electrical characterization before and after backside exposure showed no measurable degradation. Backside FA techniques including infrared (IR) imaging, thermography, laser emission microscopy (LEM), and optical beam-induced resistance change (OBIRCH) were successfully performed.        <jats:p>Three-dimensional (3D) chemical characterization at the transistor level is increasingly required for root-cause failure analysis (FA) of advanced semiconductor devices, whose yield-limiting defects are intrinsically volumetric. Multimodal electron tomography combining high-angle annular dark-field (HAADF) scanning\/transmission electron microscopy (S\/TEM) with spectroscopic chemical signal, such as energy-dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDX) or electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS), is the established route to nanometer-resolution 3D elemental volumes. The conventional workflow has remained impractical for routine FA: it relies on needle-shaped specimens, dedicated on-axis tomography holders, tilt ranges of \u00b170\u00b0 or wider, dense projection sets, fiducial-based alignment, and a sequential pipeline of manual pre-processing, reconstruction, and post-processing that consumes most of a working day per dataset. We present a self-supervised reconstruction framework that recasts multimodal electron tomography as a single inverse problem: a differentiable physical forward model is coupled with a learnable volume representation, and the 3D chemical volume, tilt-series alignment, detector response, and per-element intensity scaling are recovered jointly from raw projections without external training data, prior alignment, or per-sample tuning. Unlike classical reconstruction algorithms, the framework encodes the imaging physics of both modalities and exploits their cross-modal coupling. We demonstrate the framework on a lamella prepared by conventional FIB lift-out extracted from a semiconductor chip on a standard single-tilt holder: seven simultaneous HAADF+EDX projections within \u00b130\u00b0 yielded a segmented 3D chemical volume consistent with a reference reconstruction from 49 projections over \u00b160\u00b0, with the complete workflow completing in under two hours. Defects such as fin nano-cracks, dopant variations, interface traps, and resistive anomalies may show clear electrical signatures yet elude conventional TEM workflows. Once a TEM lamella is extracted, electrical access is lost, leaving analysts reliant on diffraction, material contrast, and spectroscopy, often in a different plane than the electrical evidence. This paper presents a TEM preparation method that preserves electrical access to planar-view and cross-section lamellae. Replacing the initial conductive deposition with an insulating dielectric keeps selected areas electrically isolated after lift-out; the standard protective layer is applied on top. Full device-level measurements are then possible on the lamella, optionally via FIB-fabricated probe pads for out-of-plane terminals. The approach supports high-precision endpointing and integrates nanoprobing, e-beam localization, and TEM analysis within a single sample. Case studies demonstrate planar-view defect localization, cross-section P-N junction mapping, and full device characterization. Probe-pad series resistance and Ga-FIB implantation, which can increase leakage, must be accounted for. The method links electrical and physical analyses in a semi-reversible manner and provides a localization pathway for backside power delivery and 3D-stacked devices, where optical fault isolation However, most automated SEM workflows rely on rule-based methods such as intensity-profile peak finders or template matching, whose fixed configuration cannot accommodate the full diversity of imaging conditions. This imposes two coupled costs that grow as conditions diversify: the lower success rate forces repeated reacquisition that consumes instrument time, while restoring accuracy requires repeated manual re-tuning that consumes operator time.      <jats:p>The increasing demand for compute power is pushing system scaling toward advanced 3D and backside-integrated architectures. While the wafer backside opens a dynamic design space with new opportunities to optimize power delivery in scaled systems, it also introduces significant challenges for failure analysis (FA). In particular, the application of traditional backside optical fault isolation techniques, widely used for decades, becomes increasingly limited at current leading-edge nodes. As a result, a variety of alternative fault-isolation techniques, including X-ray, thermal, magnetic, and electron-beam approaches, are actively used and developed. Among these, magnetic-field-based techniques enable non-invasive access to buried current paths and connectivity defects, owing to the ability of magnetic fields to propagate through insulators, semiconductors, and metals with minimal disturbance. Early implementations of magnetic field imaging in FA predominantly relied on scanning-probe approaches based on SQUID and GMR sensors.      <jats:p>Electron beam (e-beam) probing was investigated as a hardware security assessment technique for evaluating data leakage vulnerabilities in microcontrollers. Using flash memory as a case study, e-beam probing extracted data by observing internal signal lines within peripheral circuits during device operation. Critically, e-beam probing demonstrated single-acquisition capability for single event or non-periodic signals. An internal signal line at lower metal layers was identified through optical microscopy and scanning electron microscopy (SEM), then exposed using localized focus ion beam (FIB) milling. E-beam voltage probing (EVP) identified the line as one of 256 parallel output signals, enabling data extraction from the embedded flash memory. With optimized parameters, EVP extracted data from single operations without averaging. E-beam voltage imaging (EVI) provided spatial frequency mapping at 332 kHz. This study has demonstrated e-beam probing as a valuable technique for assessing data leakage vulnerabilities through internal signal paths in We propose a privacy-preserving pipeline that secures IP by heavily distorting the functional design while generating a visually realistic synthetic dataset from a small set of initial examples. A StyleGAN first learns the distribution of hardware layout masks to generate novel, macroscopically varied structures.      <jats:p>Electro-Optical Terahertz Pulse Reflectometry (EOTPR) is widely used for fault isolation in advanced semiconductor packages, where interpretation of measured waveforms relies on comparison with reference responses. These reference waveforms are traditionally generated through physics-based simulation using trace models derived from CAD layout data, a process that remains time-consuming and dependent on iterative refinement despite prior automation efforts. In this work, we present a machine learning-based approach for predicting EOTPR reference waveforms directly from CAD-derived interconnect features. The model is trained on simulation-generated reference waveforms, enabling it to learn the relationship between interconnect geometry and waveform characteristics. This eliminates the need for explicit per-signal simulation during inference and enables rapid, on-demand waveform generation. In addition, we introduce a layout-aware analysis capability that maps waveform features to interconnect geometry, enabling interactive correlation between signal-domain behavior and physical structure. The proposed framework significantly accelerates reference waveform generation while improving interpretability in failure analysis workflows.<\/jats:p>","DOI":"10.31399\/asm.cp.istfa2026p0038","type":"proceedings-article","page":"38-42","title":["ML-Predicted EOTPR Reference Waveforms from CAD with Layout-Aware Correlation for Efficient Fault Isolation in Advanced Packages"],"author":[{"given":"Arpan","family":"Bhattacherjee","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"NVIDIA Corporation , Santa Clara, CA, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]},{"given":"Joy Yiyin","family":"Liao","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"NVIDIA Corporation , Santa Clara, CA, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]}],"container-title":["International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis","ISTFA 2026: Conference Proceedings from the 52nd International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis"],"issued":{"date-parts":[[2026,10,4]]},"published":{"date-parts":[[2026,10,4]]}},{"abstract":"<jats:title>Abstract<\/jats:title>\n                  <jats:p>During a high-volume production ramp involving the transition to automated test handlers, the test floor experienced poor test socket performance, resulting in low equipment utilization due to frequent troubleshooting and maintenance. A multi-modal failure analysis investigation, combining contact resistance (CRES) mapping, compositional analysis, optical profilometry, and design iterations, identified foreign object debris (FOD) contamination and non-uniform preload due to socket deflection as key contributors to unstable contact resistance across the 1,936-pin array. Targeted improvements, including structured, data-driven online cleaning and offline maintenance processes, and an optimized socket design, dramatically increased socket insertion life, reduced socket-related retest, and improved and stabilized equipment utilization. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of an integrated electrical, materials, and mechanical characterization approach for enhancing test-socket reliability in high-volume semiconductor manufacturing.<\/jats:p>","DOI":"10.31399\/asm.cp.istfa2026p0229","type":"proceedings-article","page":"229-233","title":["Enhanced Test Socket Reliability: Multi-Modal Failure Analysis and Design Optimization for High-Volume Semiconductor Manufacturing"],"author":[{"given":"Ella","family":"Schwirzke","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"Tektronix Component Solutions , Beaverton, Oregon, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]},{"given":"Rachael","family":"Gitnes","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"Tektronix Component Solutions , Beaverton, Oregon, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]},{"given":"Christopher","family":"Maciel","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"Tektronix Component Solutions , Beaverton, Oregon, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]}],"container-title":["International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis","ISTFA 2026: Conference Proceedings from the 52nd International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis"],"issued":{"date-parts":[[2026,10,4]]},"published":{"date-parts":[[2026,10,4]]}},{"abstract":"<jats:title>Abstract<\/jats:title>\n                  <jats:p>Electrical failure analysis of mixed-signal I\/O in advanced process nodes often requires establishing an electrical connection between a hotspot or emission site identified by lock-in thermography, photon emission microscopy, or lock-in static laser stimulation to a failing top-level I\/O pin. In modern designs the emission source is frequently nested deep within hierarchical circuitry blocks, while the observable electrical failing symptom appears only on a primary I\/O interface. Manual tracing of hierarchical nets, ambiguous naming contexts, and passive interconnect elements make this correspondence labor-intensive and highly dependent on analyst experience. This work presents a method for determining whether a static electrical path exists between a source net and a target device or instance within a hierarchical netlist. The method operates on typed connectivity relations and applies a staged solver that incrementally expands the search space while constraining exploration through hierarchical alias resolution, resistor terminal expansion, lowest common ancestor (LCA) identification, and bounded device-terminal expansion. The solver terminates when an intersection is identified between source and target connectivity sets or when no valid path can be established within the defined constraints. On a production GPU design with 18 levels of hierarchy, the solver resolves path in under two minutes using a distributed database-backed implementation. The result is an ordered sequence of nets that establish connectivity between the I\/O pin and failing transistor located at the emission site.<\/jats:p>","DOI":"10.31399\/asm.cp.istfa2026p0248","type":"proceedings-article","page":"248-254","title":["Accelerated Hierarchical Netlist Correlation Between I\/O Failures and Emission Sites in Advanced Process Nodes"],"author":[{"given":"Arpan","family":"Bhattacherjee","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"NVIDIA Corporation , Santa Clara, CA, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]},{"given":"Thong","family":"Doan","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"NVIDIA Corporation , Santa Clara, CA, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]},{"given":"Joy Yin","family":"Liao","sequence":"additional","affiliation":[{"name":"NVIDIA Corporation , Santa Clara, CA, USA"}],"role":[{"vocabulary":"crossref","role":"author"}]}],"container-title":["International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis","ISTFA 2026: Conference Proceedings from the 52nd International Symposium for Testing and Failure Analysis"],"issued":{"date-parts":[[2026,10,4]]},"published":{"date-parts":[[2026,10,4]]}}],"items-per-page":1000,"query":{"start-index":0,"search-terms":null}}}
